# Data Visualization

A chart is **read by people and executed by you**. This skill turns "make it look
good" into a procedure with checks, so the result is right by construction rather
than by taste.

**The method here is design-system-agnostic.** Nothing in the procedure, the form
heuristic, the six checks, or the mark specs is specific to one product. A design
system supplies a small set of *parameters* (its ramps, a categorical order, a
diverging pair, a status palette, a texture, its surfaces, its filter components);
the method consumes them unchanged. A **validated default palette** is the
reference instance, fully specified in `references/palette.md`. To target your
brand, read that file's structure and substitute its values — touch nothing else.

> The single most important habit: **the color part is computable, so compute it.**
> Never eyeball whether a palette is colorblind-safe — run `scripts/validate_palette.js`.

## The procedure — do these in order

Color comes LAST. Most bad charts pick colors first.

1. **Pick the form.** What is the data's job — magnitude, identity, polarity, a
   single headline, change-over-time? The job picks the chart type, and sometimes
   the answer is *not a chart* (a stat tile or hero number). → `references/choosing-a-form.md`
2. **Assign color by the job it does.** Categorical (identity), sequential
   (magnitude), diverging (polarity), or status (state) — each has one rule.
   Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled. → `references/color-formula.md`
3. **VALIDATE the palette — run the script, don't reason about ΔE.**
   `node scripts/validate_palette.js "<hex,hex,…>" --mode light` (relative to
   this skill's base directory — or load it as `<script type="module">` in the
   chart's own page, where it reads
   `data-palette` off `<body>` and logs a `console.table` report). It returns
   pass/fail on the lightness band, chroma floor, adjacent-pair CVD separation,
   and contrast. Fix anything that FAILs before continuing. Re-run for
   `--mode dark` with that mode's surface.
4. **Apply mark specs & spacers.** Thin marks, 4px rounded data-ends anchored to
   the baseline, 2px lines, ≥8px markers, a 2px surface gap between fills (stacked
   segments and adjacent bars alike) and a 2px surface ring on overlapping marks,
   selective direct labels. → `references/marks-and-anatomy.md`
5. **Add the hover layer — by default.** An HTML/SVG chart *is* interactive; ship
   a crosshair+tooltip on line/area and a per-mark hover tooltip on bar/dot/cell.
   The only form that skips it is a bare stat tile with no plot. Hit targets bigger
   than the mark; filters in one row above the charts. → `references/interaction.md`
6. **Final accessibility pass.** For ≥ 2 series a legend is always present and ≤ 4
   are also direct-labeled (a single series needs no legend box — the title names
   it), so identity is never color-alone; a table view exists; dark mode is **selected** — its own
   steps from the same ramps, validated against the dark surface, not an automatic
   flip; texture is available for the CVD/print/forced-colors case.
7. **Render it and look at it.** The validator checks color, not layout — open or
   screenshot the output and eyeball it for label collisions, geometry, and overflow
   before calling it done.

Then check the result against **`references/anti-patterns.md`** — it is the catalog
of what goes wrong. If your chart matches an entry, it's wrong.

## Non-negotiables (true in every design system)

- **Assign categorical hues in fixed order, never cycled.** A 9th series is never a
  generated hue — it folds into "Other," small multiples, or composite encoding.
- **One axis.** Never a dual-axis chart (two y-scales). Two measures of different
  scale → two charts, small multiples, or indexed to a common base. *(This is the
  #1 chart mistake — see anti-patterns.)*
- **Color follows the entity, never its rank.** A filter that changes the series
  count must not repaint the survivors.
- **Sequential = one hue, light→dark. Diverging = two hues + a neutral gray
  midpoint.** Never a rainbow; never a hue at the diverging midpoint.
- **Run the validator before shipping any categorical palette.** CVD ≥ 12 is the
  target; 8–12 is a floor that is legal ONLY with secondary encoding. A contrast WARN
  obligates visible labels or a table view — it is not dismissable.
- **Thin marks; a legend always present for ≥ 2 series (none for one), with
  selective direct labels (never a number on every point); recessive grid/axes.**
- **Text wears text tokens, never the series color** — values, labels, and legends
  stay in primary/secondary/muted ink; a colored mark beside them carries identity.
- **Status colors are reserved** (good/warning/serious/critical) and never reused
  for "series 4"; they ship with an icon + label, never color alone.

## Plugging in a design system

The method is invariant; only these parameters change per system. The reference
instance — every value filled in — is `references/palette.md`.

| Parameter | What the system provides |
|---|---|
| **Ramps** | the hue scales (named steps) the palette draws from |
| **Categorical theme** | the fixed hue order (a named theme); default + alternates |
| **Sequential hue** | the default single hue for magnitude |
| **Diverging pair** | two warm/cool poles + a neutral midpoint |
| **Status palette** | good / warning / serious / critical — steps distinct from categorical |
| **Texture fill** | one directional hand-drawn fill, used at 45° / 135° |
| **Surfaces** | light & dark chart-surface colors (the validator needs these) |
| **Filter controls** | date-range & dimension controls (behavioral spec in `interaction.md`) |

To onboard a new system: fill those rows, feed its ramps to the validator, and let
it snap each slot to the nearest passing step. Structure and rules stay as written.

## Reference files

| File | What it answers |
|------|-----------------|
| `references/choosing-a-form.md` | Which chart type / is it even a chart? |
| `references/color-formula.md` | The four jobs, the six checks, snap-to-passing |
| `references/marks-and-anatomy.md` | Mark specs, spacers, labels, figures, hero number |
| `references/interaction.md` | Tooltips & hover, filters & time ranges |
| `references/components.md` | The pieces a chart is made of — build each in plain HTML |
| `references/anti-patterns.md` | **What goes wrong — check every chart against this** |
| `references/palette.md` | **The reference palette instance** — every parameter, filled in; swap for your brand's |
| `scripts/validate_palette.js` | Runnable six-checks validator (run it; don't eyeball) |

### Ansh  
Run the "deep-research" workflow.

Deep research harness — fan-out web searches, fetch sources, adversarially verify claims, synthesize a cited report.

When the user wants a deep, multi-source, fact-checked research report on any topic. BEFORE invoking, check if the question is specific enough to research directly — if underspecified (e.g., "what car to buy" without budget/use-case/region), ask 2-3 clarifying questions to narrow scope. Then pass the refined question as args, weaving the answers in.

Phases:
- Scope: Decompose question (from args) into 5 search angles
- Search: 5 parallel WebSearch agents, one per angle
- Fetch: URL-dedup, fetch top 15 sources, extract falsifiable claims
- Verify: 3-vote adversarial verification per claim (need 2/3 refutes to kill)
- Synthesize: Merge semantic dupes, rank by confidence, cite sources

### Alfred  
Sir, here's the industry, in plain words.

**What "P&C" and a "TPA" actually are.** Property & Casualty is just the insurance category that covers *things* — homes, cars, buildings — as opposed to life or health. When your house floods, the insurance company (the **carrier**) owes you a fixed process: answer your call, assess the damage, send someone to fix it, pay out. Here's the thing — carriers don't want to RUN that process. It's a cost center: call centers, paperwork armies, 50–100 tickets a minute during a storm. So they outsource entire *books of claims* to **Third-Party Administrators** — claims-processing companies. A carrier managing billions in claims hands chunks to 3–5 TPAs and says "you handle it." The TPA answers the homeowner's call, opens the file, coordinates everything, dispatches the **restoration contractor** (Alok's phrase: "a man with a van" — often 3–5 competing vans show up), and a desk **estimator** (a $100K/year human) prices the damage from the contractor's photos. TPA files it back, everyone gets paid.

**Why this is a goldmine.** The whole chain is humans doing repetitive coordination. Processing one claim costs a TPA ~$500 in human labor; Cozmo's agents do the same claim for ~$60. And because TPAs run on 10–20% margins, "every percentage point of efficiency is 10 percentage points of margin" — you're not selling software, you're selling margin to a P&L owner. That's why Alok only talks to **COOs and CEOs**, never IT.

**Why TPAs and not carriers.** TPAs are mostly PE-owned — hair-on-fire urgency, willing to sign annual contracts *now*. Carriers are big, slow, and stuck running AI "pilots" that never ship. So the wedge is TPAs; carriers come later, once Cozmo's dataset is undeniable. The endgame is what he calls the **loss intelligence rail** — becoming the data layer connecting all 650 carriers, 200+ TPAs, and 75K contractors, like open banking did for fintech.

**And the proof it works:** one TPA (the #2 player in its category) was spending $2–3M on this; Cozmo runs it for ~$0.5M, went live in 3 days against an incumbent vendor's 8-month failed rollout, and signed seven figures.

The deep-research agents are sweeping the internet now — TPA landscape (Sedgwick, Crawford, Alacrity...), restoration networks, the software incumbents (Xactimate/Verisk), the AI-claims startup field, and candidates for that garbled "Fatality" competitor. The moment it lands, I'll give you the enriched industry report plus the full content + DM strategy built on his engagement data. Give me a few minutes.

**What the research verified (now image-safe with citations):**
- **The TPA world runs on PE debt, and it's cracking.** Sedgwick, the world's largest TPA: $4.62B revenue, Carlyle-controlled, valued at $13.2B. And the parable: **Alacrity** — a top TPA doing 2.8M claims/yr — was bought by BlackRock for ~$600M in 2023; by Jan 2025 a debt restructuring **wiped out BlackRock's entire $560M stake** and lenders took the keys. This is *verified, cited, third-party*. Every TPA COO lives under exactly this margin pressure. That's Alok's opening.
- **Cozmo's own job post revealed his second GTM lane:** contractor-first — restoration companies at **$1–4K/month, live in ~7 days**, ServPro/Belfor as named targets, "$46B TPA market attacked from the contractor up." So we have two pipelines: TPA COOs (7-figure, slow, his meetings) and restoration owners (fast closes — **month-1 scoreboard wins live here**).
- **His public narrative is stale everywhere** — YC page still says "financial services." The P&C story exists only in his mouth. LinkedIn relaunch = the correction. First-mover on his own pivot.
- Still open: the "Fatality" competitor (unidentified — ask him on WhatsApp), Allianz partnership (his claim, zero corroboration — confirm before amplifying).

**Content strategy — five post archetypes, mapped to his proven formulas:**
1. **The relaunch anchor** (his 582-reaction formula): the full arc — war broke the business, one-way ticket, seven-figure deal in 3 months. Un-posted, milestone-class.
2. **PE-squeeze teardowns WITH him in frame**: "BlackRock lost $560M on a claims TPA. I know why." — verified receipts + his $500→$60 answer. Radical, cited, buyer-relevant.
3. **Speed receipts**: 2 hours vs 8 months, live in 3 days, 2-minute demo — proof posts with soft DM CTAs.
4. **Man-with-a-van stories**: the absurdity of the human claims chain, taught vividly — makes TPA operators feel seen, makes him THE voice of the category.
5. **Team/hiring energy** (his sleeper format) — US hiring posts, "avengers" style.

**DM strategy — two lanes, one engine:**
- **Lane A (TPA COOs/CEOs, his 200-list):** multi-threaded (CEO+COO+VPs — one no ≠ company no), opener references *their* margin math, never a pitch — goal is a 10-minute meeting; Alok flies. 20/day.
- **Lane B (restoration owners):** velocity lane — "you're missing calls right now; live in 7 days, $1–4K/mo" — faster yes, feeds the scoreboard, builds his loss-data rail. 30/day + comment-mining his story posts against target companies.
- Inbound: real-time reply engine — his biggest deal started as an inbound DM.

Say the word and I ship batch v2 (the five archetypes as real drafts) plus both DM scripts tonight. And I still need from Alok: 200-list, Instantly login, competitor spelling.

### Alfred  
Reference analysis done and saved to [reference-playbook.md](../Snowball/clients/nuha-hashem/references/reference-playbook.md), sir. The distilled model:

**Rebryk's machine (the priority):** 4-5 posts/week across four buckets — ~40% fast news-relevance with hard numbers ("SpaceX locked Google into $920M/month"), ~30% saveable value assets (stacks, lists, comment-gated docs pulling 1,000-5,400 comments — his follower engine), ~20% personal proof arcs (his 3K-9K viral layer), ~10% tool how-tos. Key mechanics: a number in every hook, media on every post, proven formats recycled every 6-10 weeks, and the Fluently plug woven mid-post as a credibility clause, never a CTA.

**Hopkins adds one killer move:** the recurring weekly digest franchise. Nobody owns "This week in AI agents" the way she owns voice — that's Nuha's to take, and she actually ships agents in production.

**Nuha's mix per your spec:** 40% agent-economy news in Cozmo's radius / 30% builder value assets / 30% her story + company (her proven 120-370-reaction layer becomes the trust anchor).

Transcription is on the final two chunks — the moment it lands I'll read her call, fold her real story in, and ship the rebuilt strategy + the new batch.

> SPEC.md
# Data-GIF — Studio · Visuals
> Turn an already-approved static data infographic (or any post visual with a hero element) into a seamless looping GIF where the hero MOVES — and derive every single frame from the approved static's own pixels, so the motion never changes the design.

## What it does
Animates the hero of a data visual — the donut arcs draw in, bars slide up, pills wipe in — then it holds and breathes, then retracts in a seamless loop, to catch the scroll. The static PNG is the design + approval artifact; the GIF is the final deliverable. The law: **the approved static IS the GIF.** Frames are not re-rendered in HTML/SVG; they are reconstructions of the original image's own pixels under a moving reveal mask. The hold frame is pixel-identical to the approved static.

Ap
> ```

# LinkedIn Post Search — Programmatic Options (verified July 2026)

## Headline findings

- **You already own the fastest option.** Unipile's `POST /api/v1/linkedin/search` supports `category: "posts"` with keywords, date filters, and sort — and returns engagement counters. Zero new signup needed.
- **No provider sorts by engagement server-side.** LinkedIn's own search only sorts by relevance/date. Every tool inherits that. The viral-discovery pattern is: pull N posts by keyword (sorted by relevance or date-filtered) → rank client-side by `reaction_counter + comment_counter`.
- **tomquirk/linkedin-api's GitHub repo is dead** (404s as of today — removed/taken private; PyPI still serves v2.3.1 from Nov 2024). LinkedIn's legal offensive continued through 2025-26 (Proxycurl killed Jul 2025, ProAPIs sued/settled 2026). Cookie-based tools are radioactive for accounts you care about.

---

## 1. Apify actors (you have a token — these are turnkey)

| Actor slug | Input | Engagement in output | Price | Cookies? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **`harvestapi/linkedin-post-search`** | keyword (Boolean supported), `sortBy` relevance/date, `postedLimit` (1h/24h/week/month/3mo/6mo/yr) or exact date, author/company/industry/content-type filters, pagination | Yes — reaction counts by type, comment counts; optional reactor/commenter scraping | **$2/1k posts** (drops to ~$1.50/1k on higher Apify tiers) | **No** |
| **`apimaestro/linkedin-posts-search-scraper-no-cookies`** | `keyword` (quotes = exact match), `sort_type` relevance/date_posted, `date_filter` (1h/24h/week/month), company/industry/job-title URN filters, up to 10k posts auto-paginated | Yes — reactions by type + comment counts | $5/1k | **No** |
| **`benjarapi/linkedin-post-search`** | keyword OR full LinkedIn search URL, date/content-type/sort filters, author industry/company | Yes — total reactions + per-type + comments + reposts | $3/1k | Not required |
| **`curious_coder/linkedin-post-search-scraper`** | search URLs, profile/company URLs, post URLs | Yes — deep (comment text, reactor profiles) | $30/mo rental + usage | **Yes — your session cookie = ban risk** |
| **`harvestapi/linkedin-profile-posts`** | profile/company URLs, `maxPosts`, `postedLimit`, toggle reactions/comments scraping | Yes — full breakdown | $1.50/1k posts | **No** |

HarvestAPI is the standout: 4.94★, ~20k users, 100% success rate, no cookies (they run their own infra — your accounts never touch it). They also have a standalone API + SDK ([github.com/HarvestAPI/harvestapi-sdk](https://github.com/HarvestAPI/harvestapi-sdk)) but the Apify channel is the zero-setup path since you hold a token.

**Top-creator discovery:** run `harvestapi/linkedin-post-search` on niche keywords over `postedLimit: month` → aggregate authors by summed engagement → feed winners into `harvestapi/linkedin-profile-posts` for their full recent output.

## 2. RapidAPI

- **`rockapis/linkedin-api8`** ("Real-Time LinkedIn Scraper") — has a **Search Posts endpoint** (keyword/hashtag). Sibling: [`linkedin-data-api`](https://rapidapi.com/rockapis-rockapis-default/api/linkedin-data-api). Tiers basic/pro/ultra/mega, ~1k req/hr caps; exact prices are behind RapidAPI's JS wall — typical range $10-200/mo.
- **`freshdata/fresh-linkedin-profile-data`** + newer "Fresh LinkedIn Scraper API" — `/api/v1/user/posts`, `/api/v1/company/posts`, `/api/v1/search/people`; engagement metrics included. Credit-based; post search is weaker than profile-posts here.
- Verdict: viable fallback, but a new subscription + unverifiable pricing makes it slower to start than Apify. Check tiers at the hub pages directly.

## 3. GitHub libraries

- **`tomquirk/linkedin-api`** — repo **404s** (confirmed via GitHub API today). PyPI `linkedin-api` v2.3.1 (Nov 2024) still installs; docs claim "get and search people, companies, jobs, posts." Auth = real account credentials → same-account ban risk, now unmaintained. Forks (`JNYH/linkedin_api` 2022, `PipesNBottles/li_scrapi` 2024) are stale. **Skip.**
- No credible newer OSS post-search lib surfaced — the ecosystem moved to paid no-cookie APIs after the 2025 lawsuits.

## 4. Proxycurl and successors

- **Proxycurl: dead** — shut down July 4, 2025 after LinkedIn's federal lawsuit; team pivoted to NinjaPear (no LinkedIn scraping). [Founder's post-mortem](https://nubela.co/blog/goodbye-proxycurl/).
- **EnrichLayer** — Proxycurl-style successor (mirrors endpoint/credit model). Person Search + Person Profile (can include posts), but **no post keyword search**.
- **ScrapIn** — [Person Posts endpoint](https://docs.scrapin.io/endpoint/Persons/Activities/Posts): last ~50 posts per profile URL. Enrichment tool, not discovery.
- **Lix** — posts API for user/company posts by URL; no keyword search.

## 5. Bright Data / PhantomBuster / TexAu

- **Bright Data** — [LinkedIn Scraper API](https://docs.brightdata.com/datasets/scrapers/linkedin/introduction): posts discovered **by profile/company URL**, not keyword. $1.50/1k records, 5k free/mo. Good for bulk profile-posts, not search.
- **PhantomBuster** — $69-439/mo, runs on **your session cookie**, documented bans in 2026. Post-search phantoms exist but worst risk/cost ratio here.
- **TexAu** — **retired its LinkedIn automations in 2026** pending v3. Off the table.

## 6. Niche players

- **HarvestAPI** (harvestapi.io, site geo-blocks direct fetch) — the same team behind the top Apify actors; standalone API + SDK if you outgrow Apify. Best price-per-post in the market ($1.50-2/1k).

---

## Top 3 fastest-to-use TODAY

1. **Unipile `linkedin/search` with `category: "posts"`** — creds already live. Params: `keywords`, `sort_by: "date"`, `date_posted: "past_week"`, `content_type`, `author`. Returns `reaction_counter`, `comment_counter`, `repost_counter`, `impressions_counter` + text/author. Cost: already paid. Risk: consumes a connected real account's search quota — run it through a non-critical profile, stay under a few hundred results/day. [Docs](https://developer.unipile.com/docs/linkedin-search).
2. **Apify `harvestapi/linkedin-post-search`** — token already held; one HTTP call to run. $2/1k, no cookies (zero risk to your accounts), Boolean keywords + date windows + per-type reactions. Best for volume pulls (1k+ posts per niche).
3. **Apify `apimaestro/linkedin-posts-search-scraper-no-cookies`** — same zero-setup, adds URN-level filters (author industry/company/title) HarvestAPI lacks; use when you need "posts by VCs only" style slicing. $5/1k.

Recommended architecture: Unipile for cheap interactive queries, HarvestAPI actor for bulk reference-library builds, engagement ranking done in Python on the merged output.

Sources: [harvestapi/linkedin-post-search](https://apify.com/harvestapi/linkedin-post-search) · [apimaestro actor](https://apify.com/apimaestro/linkedin-posts-search-scraper-no-cookies) · [benjarapi actor](https://apify.com/benjarapi/linkedin-post-search) · [curious_coder actor](https://apify.com/curious_coder/linkedin-post-search-scraper) · [harvestapi/linkedin-profile-posts](https://apify.com/harvestapi/linkedin-profile-posts) · [HarvestAPI SDK](https://github.com/HarvestAPI/harvestapi-sdk) · [linkedin-api8](https://rapidapi.com/rockapis-rockapis-default/api/linkedin-api8) · [Fresh LinkedIn Profile Data](https://rapidapi.com/freshdata-freshdata-default/api/fresh-linkedin-profile-data) · [linkedin-api PyPI](https://pypi.org/project/linkedin-api/) · [Proxycurl shutdown](https://nubela.co/blog/goodbye-proxycurl/) · [ProAPIs suit](https://thelinkedblog.com/2026/linkedin-reaches-deal-in-data-scraping-lawsuit-against-proapis-3857/) · [ScrapIn Person Posts](https://docs.scrapin.io/endpoint/Persons/Activities/Posts) · [Lix posts](https://lix-it.com/blog/linkedin-posts-api/) · [Bright Data docs](https://docs.brightdata.com/datasets/scrapers/linkedin/introduction) · [PhantomBuster pricing review](https://puzzleinbox.com/compare/phantombuster-pricing-review) · [TexAu review](https://connectsafely.ai/articles/texau-review-linkedin-automation-2026) · [Unipile LinkedIn search](https://developer.unipile.com/docs/linkedin-search)</result>

### Alfred  
Full sweep done, sir. The toolbox is saved at [product/reference-discovery-toolbox.md](../Snowball/product/reference-discovery-toolbox.md) — here's the landscape:

**Validated live, usable this minute, already paid for:**
1. **Unipile posts-search** — our own API has a hidden weapon: keyword search of LinkedIn posts with date filters and engagement counts. Tested, $0.
2. **Apify `harvestapi/linkedin-post-search`** — the market's best no-cookie scraper ($2/1k posts, zero risk to our accounts). Tested with our token, correct schema mapped.
3. **Our deep profile scraper** — already proven on Rebryk/Hopkins/Nuha/Alok.

**Key market fact:** LinkedIn's 2025-26 legal war killed most of this space — Proxycurl dead, Kleo's extension C&D'd, SHIELD shutting down, the classic Python lib gone. Cookie-based tools (PhantomBuster) are ban-bait and never touch client accounts. Nobody sorts by engagement server-side — we rank client-side, which our engine already does.

**SaaS worth free trials:** **Taplio** (5M viral-post library with an API *and* an MCP — Claude can query it directly, ~$65/mo), **Favikon** (the only per-niche creator ranking engine — its Top-200 niche pages are free to browse), **MagicPost** ($39/mo with API), **TypeGrow** (free 1M library).

**The engine this unlocks:** discover by niche keyword → rank by engagement → aggregate the winning authors → deep-scrape them → distill copy formulas. The Rebryk treatment, automated, for any niche.

Say the word and I run it for Nuha's niches — AI agents, enterprise AI, insurance tech — and come back with the top creators and reference posts feeding straight into her next batch.

Topic bank built off what her data actually proves, sir — her winners are broad-tech stories with her builder's take (Opus 5, Gemini, burger/water, Attention paper), and the insurance-forward stuff is exactly what she skipped or what historically flopped. Ten new topics, mapped to her three proven formulas:

**News & analysis (the 40% lane):**

1. **"Every AI lab is quietly becoming a consulting company"** — OpenAI's $4B deployment venture, Anthropic + Blackstone's $1.5B Ode, Accenture's AI billions (all verified in our research runs). Her take: the labs discovered models don't deploy themselves — and she's been living that truth for 4 years. 
2. **"Nobody benchmarks their electricity"** — her own line from the Gemini post, promoted to a full thesis: AI is leaving the magic era and entering the infrastructure era, and what that means for builders. Extends two already-approved posts.
3. **"The smartest teams are switching to dumber models"** — the small-model countertrend: distillation, on-device, cost-per-task beating raw IQ. Fact-check at draft time for current examples.
4. **"Memory, not intelligence, is the bottleneck"** — why every agent demo dies at conversation 12. Her literal specialty (context, orchestration), framed for any builder, zero insurance.

**Saveable value (the 30% lane):**

5. **The energy myth-bust** — direct sibling of the burger/water post she loved ("the prompts thing"): what one AI query actually costs in electricity vs your kettle, with the same read-the-actual-studies arithmetic. 
6. **"How to read a research paper in 30 minutes"** — the PhD-dropout's method, landing on "the next Attention paper is sitting unread right now." Pairs with her approved 15-page-PDF post; maximum save-test.
7. **"COBOL still runs your money"** — the 60-year-old code underneath the AI economy, told with affection and real numbers. Lets her talk about ancient enterprise software (her daily reality) without saying the word insurance.
8. **"The best AI feature we ever shipped is a pause button"** — her Approvals story generalized into an agent-design principle for any builder: autonomy is easy, knowing when to stop is the product.

**Story & company (the 30% lane):**

9. **"I spend 3 hours a day not coding, and it's why we ship faster"** — the 79-customer-calls discipline as an engineering tool, not a sales brag.
10. **"My co-founder broke my wrist" reprise, US edition** — the Saturday 4x4 ritual as conflict-resolution infrastructure; her proven culture-post lane, real photos available.

Every one passes her guardrails (no politics, no family, no client names, she's in frame in all ten) and each carries a fact-check note for the gate before drafting. Want me to queue these into her topics file and draft the first four in the locked register?

### Ansh  
Our customers pay us more because our AI asks permission.

I fought this idea for 2 years.

I used to sell what everyone in AI sells, which is an AI that handles everything by itself.

Then we built the opposite, and I watched it outsell everything we made before. Before any important decision, our AI stops, shows a human exactly what it is about to do, and waits for a yes, which costs the human 1 tap.

I sat in a review where a leader at one of the biggest companies we work with said this single feature changed how their organization sees AI, and I then watched them roll it out to 8 more departments because of it.

I now decide what the AI can do alone using 2 questions, and I ask them about every single action:

1. Can I undo this action if it turns out wrong

Let's try to perfect this post first and send it across to Nuha.

**Do the changes make sense? Yes — and one of them fixes an error that was mine, not hers.**

1. **"2 years → 6 months" is a factual correction disguised as a taste note.** Cozmo only became an AI company in 2025, and Approvals shipped about five weeks before her April post. A two-year internal fight never fit their real timeline — I inherited "2 years" from an earlier draft's framing and she's the only person who actually knows how long she resisted. Beyond accuracy, it reads better: six months of resistance is healthy engineering skepticism, two years is stubbornness. For a CTO whose whole brand is precision, the true number is the stronger number.

2. **Keeping the ending is the right reversal.** Her first instinct ("we can end it differently") was probably the same worry you flagged earlier — that vulnerability reads as weakness. But by the end of the post, competence is already proven: millions of calls handled, a Fortune 500 expanding to 8 workflows. Against that backdrop, "We shipped it scared" isn't self-deprecation, it's the one honest human line in a story about trust — and it's the line people will quote in the comments. She saw that on second look, and she's right.

The encouraging meta-signal, sir: she's engaging at the level of individual words now, not rejecting concepts. That's taste-training data, and it means the format war is over. The post is ship-ready — the moment her LinkedIn is connected, this goes first.

3. Files and Code Sections:
- `clients/nuha-hashem/drafts/2026-08-07-t08-pause-button.md` — THE current pilot post, v8 final. Full body:
```
Our customers pay us more because our AI does less...

and I fought that idea for 2 years.

Our AI employees at Cozmo AI (YC W22) answer insurance phone calls for real companies. The 2 am calls, after storms and floods, from stressed people watching water drip through their ceiling.

When we started, I believed our job was full autonomy, meaning an AI that handles the whole call, the claim, and the follow-up with no human anywhere. We got genuinely good at that, and our system has now handled millions of these calls.

Then last year we shipped a feature that felt like going backwards.

Before any decision that matters, like approving a payment or making a promise to a customer, our AI stops, shows a human exactly what it is about to do, and waits for a yes. One tap approves it, and one tap corrects it.

We called it Approvals, and I honestly worried it made us look weaker than competitors promising full autonomy.

A few weeks later I sat in a review with one of the biggest companies we work with, ready to defend our roadmap.

Their MD pointed at Approvals instead.

"This changed how we see AI."

They expanded it to 8 more workflows after that meeting.

Here is the rule I took away, and I now apply it to every action our AI can take:

1. A mistake that is cheap and reversible: the AI acts alone and logs it.
2. A mistake that is expensive and permanent: the AI asks a human first.
3. The ask-first list stays tiny, about 1 action in 20, so a yes still means someone truly looked.

The part I never expected is that the pause made us faster, because once people saw the AI knew when to ask, they stopped double-checking everything else it did.

I would love to tell you this was the plan all along.

We shipped it scared :)
```

Open in Safari, sir — [nuha-pause-post-2026-08-08](https://app.thesnowball.agency/preview-linkedin/nuha-pause-post-2026-08-08). This is the finalized v10 with both her WhatsApp edits in ("6 months" hook, new customers-pushed-us ending). Full text as it stands:

> Our customers pay us more because our AI does less...
>
> and I fought that idea for 6 months.
>
> Our AI employees at Cozmo AI (YC W22) answer insurance phone calls for real companies. The 2 am calls, after storms and floods, from stressed people watching water drip through their ceiling.
>
> When we started, I believed our job was full autonomy, meaning an AI that handles the whole call, the claim, and the follow-up with no human anywhere. We got genuinely good at that, and our system has now handled millions of these calls.
>
> Then last year we shipped a feature that felt like going backwards.
>
> Before any decision that matters, like approving a payment or making a promise to a customer, our AI stops, shows a human exactly what it is about to do, and waits for a yes. One tap approves it, and one tap corrects it.
>
> We called it Approvals, and I honestly worried it made us look weaker than competitors promising full autonomy.
>
> A few weeks later I sat in a review with one of the biggest companies we work with, ready to defend our roadmap.
>
> Their MD pointed at Approvals instead.
>
> "This changed how we see AI."
>
> They expanded it to 8 more workflows after that meeting.
>
> Here is the rule I took away, and I now apply it to every action our AI can take:
>
> 1. A mistake that is cheap and reversible: the AI acts alone and logs it.
> 2. A mistake that is expensive and permanent: the AI asks a human first.
> 3. The ask-first list stays tiny, about 1 action in 20, so a yes still means someone truly looked.
>
> The part I never expected is that the pause made us faster, because once people saw the AI knew when to ask, they stopped double-checking everything else it did.
>
> I would love to tell you this was the plan all along.
>
> The truth is our customers pushed us there, and I am glad we listened :)

Media: her real photo. Status: ship-ready the moment her LinkedIn connects.

> Every week I see a post saying AI is drinking the planet. One bottle of water per question.
>
> The real number is about 0.3 millilitres per question. That is 5 drops, and Google and OpenAI both report the same range.
>
> The viral bottle figure? The researchers behind it later revised it down to about 15 millilitres. The correction never trended.
>
> Now the burger. Producing 1 burger takes about 2,500 litres of water, and a pair of jeans takes 7,000.
>
> So you would need about 8 million prompts to match 1 lunch.
>
> I care about this because our AI at Cozmo AI (YC W22) answers millions of insurance calls. If the myth were true, we would be draining a lake every month.
>
> The water problem is real in one specific way. Data centres strain the towns they get built in, so the issue is where they are built, not whether you ask your question.
>
> Before you feel guilty about a prompt, check 3 things:
>
> 1. The unit. Water per question sounds scary until you see it is 5 drops.
> 2. The baseline. Compare it with your lunch, not with zero.
> 3. The location. Where a data centre stands matters more than how often you use it.
>
> Your prompt was never the problem.
>
> Enjoy the burger :)

Rewritten and [live in Safari](https://app.thesnowball.agency/preview-linkedin/nuha-burger-post-2026-08-08), sir. I went through all 10 e-series posts line by line first, and the diagnosis was embarrassing but clear: Alok's posts are full of contractions and sentences merged with "because/so/and" — my draft had *zero* contractions and chopped everything into full-stop fragments. That alone was the AI smell.

What the v7 does differently:

- **Your hook verbatim**, then the myth as one flowing sentence with the quote embedded: *"Every week I see a post saying 'AI is drinking the planet' and that every question you ask costs a bottle of water."*
- **"Actually, a question costs about 0.3 mL..."** — your exact fix, no "the real number is" anywhere.
- **Contractions throughout**: we'd, isn't, didn't, you'd, I'd, they're.
- **One rhetorical pivot** ("So where did the bottle story come from?"), **one listicle** with the Alok-style intro ("this is what I'd check:"), asides folded into sentences instead of standing alone.
- **Twist close kept**: "Your prompt was never the problem. / Enjoy the burger :)"

All facts untouched from the verified research. The mechanics are now written into her copy-formula as a permanent lock — zero contractions = instant rejection before anything reaches you.

    return hmac.new(secret.encode(), f"preview-edit:{slug}".encode(),
                    hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()[:24]

The export is unpacked and read — the Aug 10 burst (line 516 onward) is a goldmine, sir. Before the topics, two things from the chat that change standing rules:

- **Canonical company description changed.** Alok, Aug 11: *"We are building OS for property claims by automating the front and back office for restoration networks, adjusters and TPAs."* He explicitly killed "voice agents for P&C" ("like saying we are building excel sheets for accountants"). I'm writing this into both clients' memory files — it changes how every future post describes Cozmo, on both accounts.
- **Alok gave the same AI-tell feedback as Nuha** (line 592: "these 3 lines have AI tell"). Both founders now police this — the verb ban is officially account-wide.

**The 7 common topics for Nuha's account:**

1. **The go-live nobody believes** — this week they took a customer live integrated with **4 systems of record, under 2% error rate, processing 50% of daily claims intake**. Her CTO angle: the hard part was never the AI, it was making an agent drive 40-year-old software reliably. Flagship engineering receipt; ties to her COBOL topic (t07).

2. **First computer-vision back-office agents in the industry** — agents that operate *any* system, API or no API, by seeing the screen. This is the product-launch post, and it's the new "OS for property claims" one-liner in action.

3. **$32K cloud bill to below zero** — 3 months of spend erased via startup cloud programs. Practical CTO money post; every founder reading saves real dollars. Pure value lane.

4. **Hiring, her version** — Alok already agreed to two versions ("one in yours and another from nuha's :)"). 4 roles, 5 people, blunt bar (technical, startup-hardened, ~6-7 days). Her pixel hiring image is already built and unused.

5. **The World Cup mask** (photo 3287 — the two of them at FIFA 2026 with the Egyptian flag, his face painted) — her Indian co-founder, who doesn't follow soccer, chanted "Masr Masr" until his throat gave out, straight after 8 hours of client meetings. Cofounder-trust story only she can post. Real photo, both in frame.

6. **The cardboard-box beach story** (photo 3263) — their first company, Zywa: three founders on a Dubai beach wearing cardboard signs to win their first 10 users. From her: "before we sold AI to insurance companies, we wore boxes on a beach." Shared origin, incredible real artifact.

7. **Planes over pipelines** (photos 3270/3296) — a new city every week, Kansas City next, breaking into a legacy industry at conferences instead of cold email. Her twist: what the conference floor taught her that dashboards couldn't. (I'd use the plane/airport photos — the conference selfie has a third party in frame, which our exposure rule blocks.)

**Bench:** Saturday all-hands + soccer + Cookie the mascot (photos 3266/3265) — and note, this confirms her "4 on 4" game is **soccer**, so t10's court-and-shot language needs correcting before it ever ships.

Say the word and I'll build all 7 on the full treatment, previews ready before her tomorrow-morning slot — nothing schedules until you say so.
